28:I[10790,["/_next/static/chunks/3rdqn8rx95pw7.js","/_next/static/chunks/2qa86i-3sn8fb.js"],"LessonComplete"]
29:I[93060,["/_next/static/chunks/3rdqn8rx95pw7.js","/_next/static/chunks/2qa86i-3sn8fb.js"],"LessonToc"]
2a:T4dd,Every algorithm through Week 8 is model-free: it learns a policy or value function directly from environment interaction, without representing how the environment works. Model-free methods are powerful but data-hungry — each transition contributes one gradient step, and the environment must be queried millions of times before useful behavior emerges. Model-based RL breaks that dependence: the agent learns, or is given, a model of the environment's dynamics. With a model, it can imagine the consequences of actions without executing them, generating synthetic experience to supplement or replace real interaction. This is the computational analog of mentally simulating a trajectory before moving — and it matters most for physical systems, where every real interaction carries a cost or safety risk. Model-based RL is not one algorithm but a design space. The model can be learned or given; planning can consume it for one step, a short horizon, or a whole tree of possible futures; and the model can live in observation space or in a learned latent space. We work through that space from simple to complex, anchoring each method to classical control theory (MPC, from the robotics course) and to modern systems that plan before acting.2b:T6a0,The Dyna architecture (Sutton, 1990) is the prototypical integration of model learning and model-free value estimation. At each real environment step: 1. Execute , observe ; update model . 2. Update from the real transition (standard TD). 3. Imagined rollouts ( iterations): Sample a random previously visited state and action . Query model: , . Update from the imagined transition. Each real step now funds value updates — one real, imagined — without additional real-world interaction. For an accurate model, an imagined transition is as informative as a real one, so Dyna with effectively performs a hundred value updates per real step, at the cost of model evaluations. The conceptual contribution of Dyna is establishing that model-based and model-free learning are complementary, not competing: the model improves value estimates between real steps, and better value estimates guide smarter real-world data collection. This "imagination loop" — generate imagined experience, learn from it, act to collect better real data — recurs throughout model-based RL and in modern AI systems that deliberate before acting. real env 1 real step learned model P̂, R̂ imagined rollouts k samples value update Q real + imagined update model query learn act better Dyna: one real step funds one real plus k imagined value updates } alt={The Dyna loop: real steps update a model, imagined rollouts update values`}> Dyna interleaves model learning with value estimation: each real transition updates the model and , and imagined transitions from the model fund extra value updates at no real-world cost. The imagined-rollout loop is the conceptual core of every model-based method in this lecture.2c:T5ce,In continuous action spaces, gradient-based optimization through differentiable models is possible. For non-differentiable models or non-smooth rewards, sampling-based optimizers are standard: Cross-Entropy Method (CEM): sample action sequences from a Gaussian distribution over action sequences, evaluate the total return for each using model rollouts, select the top- sequences (the "elite" set ), and refit the distribution: Iterating this refit-sample cycle concentrates the sampling distribution on high-return regions. CEM is simple, parallelizable, and effective for horizons up to in continuous control, though it can collapse prematurely to a local optimum if the elite fraction is too small. MPPI (Model Predictive Path Integral): computes a principled gradient-free update by weighting action trajectories according to exponentiated returns. For a control problem with running cost , MPPI samples noise sequences and computes the update: where is the temperature controlling the soft-maximum. MPPI solves a KL-regularized version of the stochastic optimal control problem: it seeks the control distribution closest to the previous one that achieves lower expected cost. The exponential weighting ensures that trajectories with much lower cost contribute exponentially more to the update. MPPI is widely used in autonomous driving (steering + throttle control under nonlinear vehicle dynamics) and legged locomotion (real-time replanning for foot placement on uneven terrain).2d:T6f1,Monte Carlo Tree Search (MCTS) builds a search tree rooted at the current state by running many simulated trajectories, each one a pass through four steps: Selection: starting from the root, repeatedly pick the child action that maximizes a PUCT (Predictor + Upper Confidence bound for Trees) score, where is the current estimated value of taking from , is the prior probability assigned to , is the visit count of , and is the visit count of the edge . The first term favors actions already known to be good; the second term favors actions the prior likes but that have been tried rarely (low inflates the bonus). Descend edges this way until reaching a node not yet in the tree — a leaf. Expansion: add the leaf to the tree and populate its outgoing edges with a prior over actions — for MuZero, this prior comes from the learned policy network rather than a hand-designed heuristic. Evaluation: obtain a value estimate for the leaf. Classic MCTS (e.g. AlphaGo's predecessor systems) evaluates via random rollout to a terminal state; MuZero instead queries its learned value network directly at the leaf, avoiding any rollout. Backup: propagate the leaf's value back up every edge on the path to the root, incrementing each edge's visit count and updating its running average to include the new estimate. Repeating this loop hundreds or thousands of times per real decision concentrates simulations on promising lines of play while still sampling weaker-looking ones enough to correct early misjudgments. After the simulation budget is exhausted, the root's visit counts , normalized into a distribution, form the improved policy — search consistently produces a better policy than the raw prior because it looks ahead rather than committing to the prior's first guess.2e:T54d,MuZero (Schrittwieser et al., 2020) represents a conceptually distinct approach: learn a model whose sole purpose is to support the MCTS loop above, without any obligation to decode back to observations. MuZero's three learned functions: | Function | Input → Output | Role | |---|---|---| | Representation | Observation history → | Encode real observation | | Dynamics | → | Latent transitions + reward | | Prediction | → | Policy and value from latent | At each real step, MCTS is run entirely within the latent space generated by repeated application of . The tree is expanded using to guide search and to estimate leaf returns. The resulting action visit counts form the improved policy , which is both executed and used as a training target for . The training signal for comes from temporal consistency of predictions along real trajectories — not observation reconstruction. MuZero's latent representation may bear no resemblance to the physical state; it only needs to support accurate value and policy prediction for MCTS. This demonstrates that observation-space accuracy is neither necessary nor sufficient for effective planning: a model with low reconstruction error but poor value prediction is useless for RL; a model with accurate value prediction on abstract latents supports excellent planning regardless of reconstruction quality.2f:T435,| Algorithm | Model space | Rollout | Planner | Strength | |---|---|---|---|---| | Dyna / MBPO | Observation | 1–5 steps | Q-learning / SAC | Sample efficiency, simplicity | | MPC + CEM | Observation | 10–50 steps | CEM sampling | Constraints, no learned value fn | | MPC + terminal | Observation | 5–20 steps | CEM + bootstrap | Balances model and value bias | | Dreamer (RSSM) | Learned latent | Full episode | Analytic actor gradient | Visual control, sample efficiency | | MuZero | Abstract latent | MCTS tree | MCTS | Discrete games, combinatorial tasks | All model-based methods share one tradeoff: sample efficiency against model-accuracy demands. Model-free methods need many real transitions; model-based methods need fewer but demand an accurate model — a different kind of engineering (architecture choices, training stability, validation). The right choice depends on whether real-world interaction is the bottleneck (favor model-based) or whether learning a model is itself difficult or unsafe (favor model-free, or model-based with uncertainty penalties).30:T4a6,The model-based RL perspective provides precise language for recent LLM developments. An LLM generating a chain-of-thought runs a one-step greedy rollout: predict the next token conditioned on all previous tokens, commit. This is the model-free degenerate case — prediction without planning. Tree of Thoughts (ToT) introduces genuine planning: the LLM is simultaneously the dynamics model (predicting consequences of reasoning steps) and the value/policy network (evaluating which branch is promising). MCTS is run in reasoning-step space, exactly as in MuZero with the LLM in the model role. The latent state is the text context; actions are reasoning steps; value estimates come from self-critique. Best-of-N sampling with process reward models (PRMs) is MPC with CEM: generate many candidate reasoning trajectories, score each with a learned reward model, and select the best. The MBRL framework therefore predicts the effectiveness of test-time compute scaling: more planning compute (more MCTS iterations, more candidates, deeper trees) improves performance on reasoning tasks with checkable answers, such as competition mathematics and code (Snell et al., 2024; Brown et al., 2024).31:T42f,World models approximate environment dynamics and enable imagined trajectories that substitute for real interaction; the tradeoff is model accuracy against planning efficiency, and compounding error sets the reliable rollout length. Dyna established the principle of interleaving real and imagined experience; MBPO scales it to deep networks with short-horizon probabilistic rollouts. MPC re-optimizes a short-horizon plan at every step from the latest observation; its lineage from LQR and iLQR makes it the bridge between classical control and RL. A terminal value function resolves the short-horizon limitation by bootstrapping the tail of the return, trading model error against value error. Dreamer shows that planning in a learned latent space beats planning in observation space. MuZero makes the strongest claim: a model that only supports MCTS on abstract latents can outperform observation-space models with no reconstruction objective. Planning is a first-class AI capability, and the MBRL framework gives the vocabulary to reason about when and how to use it.32:T116e,1. The compounding error problem states that one-step model error can produce rollout error growing as over steps. Explain mechanistically why errors compound exponentially rather than linearly for a nonlinear model, using the concept of model state diverging from the true manifold of valid states. How does the terminal value function strategy mitigate — but not eliminate — this problem? What property must have for the mitigation to be effective? 2. MBPO uses short rollouts ( – ) starting from real replay buffer states rather than full rollouts from the initial state distribution. Explain why this reduces compounding error. What does starting rollouts from the replay buffer assume about the on-policy vs replay state distributions, and when would this assumption fail? 3. MuZero trains its dynamics model to support MCTS, not to reconstruct observations. Compare MuZero's latent with Dreamer's . For each, identify: (a) what training signal shapes the latent representation, (b) whether the latent must correspond to a physically interpretable quantity, and (c) what information would be lost if the decoder objective were removed. Under what task conditions would MuZero's representation be preferred? 4. You are designing model-based RL for a robotic arm where rigid-body dynamics are known analytically but contact dynamics are highly uncertain. Describe a hybrid strategy using an analytical model for rigid-body components and a learned residual for contact dynamics. How would you modify MPC's receding-horizon optimization to incorporate both? What must the planning horizon satisfy relative to the timescale of contact events? 5. Map Tree of Thoughts onto the MuZero architecture by identifying what corresponds to: the representation function , dynamics function , prediction function , latent state , and MCTS search. Identify at least one structural difference that makes ToT less principled from a planning theory perspective. How would you modify ToT to address this? 1. Compounding error. A one-step error nudges the predicted state slightly off the manifold of valid states; for nonlinear dynamics the next prediction is then made from an already-wrong state where the model is even less accurate, so errors feed back multiplicatively and grow like rather than additively. A terminal value function caps the rollout at short steps and bootstraps the remainder, so error accumulates only over — but only if is accurate on the reached states; it does not remove the within-horizon compounding. 2. MBPO short rollouts. Starting – rollouts from real replay states keeps model queries near states it has actually seen, bounding how far error compounds. It assumes the replay (largely off-policy) state distribution matches the current on-policy distribution; this fails once the policy has shifted substantially, so rollouts begin from states the current policy rarely visits, reintroducing distribution mismatch. 3. MuZero vs Dreamer latents. MuZero's is shaped purely by reward/value/policy-prediction losses, so (a) the training signal is value-equivalence, (b) the latent need not be physically interpretable, and (c) removing a decoder loses nothing because it never reconstructs observations — it keeps only task-relevant information. Dreamer's is shaped by a reconstruction (ELBO) objective, must encode enough to rebuild observations, and would lose its main representation signal without the decoder. MuZero's representation is preferred when observations carry lots of task-irrelevant detail. 4. Hybrid model. Use the known rigid-body model plus a learned contact residual, , and run MPC's receding-horizon optimization over the combined model. The horizon must be short enough that contact transients are resolved at the model's timescale — on the order of (or finer than) the contact-event timescale — or contact dynamics get aliased and missed. 5. Tree of Thoughts as MuZero. Representation ≈ encoding the prompt into a reasoning state; dynamics ≈ the LLM generating the next thought; prediction ≈ the heuristic value/score of a thought; latent ≈ the current partial reasoning state; MCTS ≈ ToT's tree search. The key gap: ToT's dynamics and value are uncalibrated LLM self-evaluations with no learned value-equivalence or grounding, so the search optimizes a noisy proxy. Fix by training a learned verifier/value model (and grounding steps in tool feedback) so the search targets a calibrated objective.33:T738,1. Model-ensemble uncertainty for safe planning: Extend Exercise 2's MPC by replacing the single dynamics model with an ensemble of probabilistic models. Use the ensemble disagreement (variance across predicted next states) as an uncertainty penalty in the CEM cost function. Measure whether ensemble-based uncertainty penalties reduce the frequency of catastrophic planning failures (rollouts that predict high reward but produce low reward when executed). 2. MuZero without a known simulator: Study the MuZero pseudocode and identify which components require a simulator for training (the MCTS tree expansion relies on the dynamics function , which is learned — so what's the simulator's role?). Design a fully offline MuZero variant that learns from a fixed dataset with no environment interaction at all. What new failure mode emerges? 3. MBRL for LLM reasoning: Implement a simplified Tree of Thoughts on a reasoning benchmark (e.g., GSM8K). Compare BFS-style tree expansion vs MCTS with the LLM providing both the dynamics (next-reasoning-step prediction) and the value (self-critique scoring). Measure accuracy as a function of the tree budget (number of nodes expanded). Does MCTS outperform BFS for the same compute budget? Connect your findings to the MBRL framework's prediction about test-time compute scaling. Week 11: Offline Reinforcement Learning. The next lecture addresses a fundamental practical constraint: what if the agent cannot interact with the environment at all during learning? We study learning from fixed datasets collected by a separate behavior policy — the dominant setting for safe deployment in robotics, healthcare, and AI alignment — and develop the conservative methods (CQL, IQL, AWAC) that address the distributional shift and Q-value overestimation problems that make standard RL fail on offline data.34:T4ad,Sutton, R. Learning"}]]}]}]]}]]}],["$","$L29",null,{"headings":[{"depth":2,"heading":"The case for learning a model","anchor":"the-case-for-learning-a-model","text":"$2a"},{"depth":2,"heading":"What is a world model?","anchor":"what-is-a-world-model","text":"A world model approximates the environment's transition and reward functions: Given a world model, the agent can unroll imagined trajectories: using the model in place of real environment steps. If the model is accurate, imagined trajectories carry the same learning signal as real ones at a fraction of the wall-clock cost. If it is inaccurate, they actively mislead the learner — a policy optimized against a flawed model can fail arbitrarily when deployed. Managing that trade-off between efficiency and model-induced bias is the central design challenge of model-based RL."},{"depth":3,"heading":"Deterministic vs probabilistic models","anchor":"deterministic-vs-probabilistic-models","text":"A deterministic model predicts a single next state, trained via MSE on observed transitions. It is fast and simple but cannot represent uncertainty. Errors are silent: the model produces a confident prediction even in regions where the true dynamics are stochastic or poorly constrained. A probabilistic model outputs a distribution over next states, typically . The variance doubles as a practical uncertainty estimate: high variance marks regions of sparse data or genuinely stochastic dynamics. Uncertainty-aware planning can then penalize action sequences that pass through high-variance regions. Ensemble models train independent deterministic models on different data subsets. Disagreement across ensemble predictions provides a practical epistemic uncertainty estimate. MBPO and PETS use probabilistic ensembles of Gaussian models as a sweet spot between expressivity and training cost."},{"depth":3,"heading":"The compounding error problem","anchor":"the-compounding-error-problem","text":"Errors compound over time: for nonlinear dynamics, a one-step prediction error can grow to over model steps as imagined states drift off the true manifold of valid states. The growth is multiplicative, not additive: a small per-step error nudges the imagined state into a region the model was not trained on, where its error is larger still, and that larger error compounds again on the next step — so the effective error at step scales with the product of per-step amplification factors rather than their sum, giving exponential rather than linear growth in . Even an accurate model with is not trustworthy over long horizons — 50 steps of amplified error can carry the imagined state far from anything the model has been trained on. This is the open-loop prediction problem studied in robust control. Standard mitigations: limit rollout length to the reliable horizon, replan frequently with closed-loop correction, or bootstrap long-horizon returns with a learned value function."},{"depth":2,"heading":"Dyna: interleaving real and imagined experience","anchor":"dyna-interleaving-real-and-imagined-experience","text":"$2b"},{"depth":3,"heading":"Model-Based Policy Optimization (MBPO)","anchor":"model-based-policy-optimization-mbpo","text":"MBPO (Janner et al., 2019) is the modern deep-learning incarnation of Dyna. Short model rollouts of length are generated starting from states in the real replay buffer; imagined transitions are added to a separate model buffer; and a SAC agent trains on the combined real + imagined data. Empirically, to works well across MuJoCo tasks, achieving near-state-of-the-art sample efficiency in under 500k real environment steps — compared to 2–3 million required by SAC alone."},{"depth":2,"heading":"Model Predictive Control","anchor":"model-predictive-control","text":"Model Predictive Control (MPC) is a classical control algorithm that fits cleanly into the model-based RL framework. Students of the robotics course have seen MPC for known dynamics; here it is placed in the context of learned models."},{"depth":3,"heading":"The receding horizon principle","anchor":"the-receding-horizon-principle","text":"At each time step , MPC solves: subject to , . Only the first action is executed. At the next step, the agent re-observes the true state and replans over a new horizon. This is the receding horizon structure. Why only the first action? A learned model is only an approximation, so executing the full -step plan propagates errors without correction. Replanning each step from the new true observation implements closed-loop feedback on the model: it continuously corrects for model error and unexpected perturbations (disturbances in robot control). This is why MPC tolerates model mismatch — it never commits to more than one step at a time."},{"depth":3,"heading":"Optimizers within MPC","anchor":"optimizers-within-mpc","text":"$2c"},{"depth":3,"heading":"Terminal value functions","anchor":"terminal-value-functions","text":"Finite-horizon MPC considers only returns over horizon . The standard solution adds a terminal value function estimated by a learned critic: This is the model-based analog of TD( ): the model covers short horizons with high fidelity, while the value function supplies the long-horizon return. Small leans on (high bias if the critic is inaccurate); large leans on the model (high bias if its error compounds). The optimal is where the two error sources balance."},{"depth":3,"heading":"Connection to the robotics course","anchor":"connection-to-the-robotics-course","text":"The model-based RL design space is the generalization of classical control methods: | Setting | Algorithm | |---|---| | Known linear dynamics, quadratic cost | LQR / DARE (Bellman in closed form) | | Known nonlinear dynamics | iLQR, Differential Dynamic Programming | | Unknown dynamics, learned model | MBRL with MPC | | Unknown dynamics, model-free | SAC, PPO | The progression from LQR to MBRL is from known to unknown, analytical to data-driven, and exact to approximate. All are solving the same problem — optimal sequential decision-making under dynamics constraints — with different information assumptions."},{"depth":2,"heading":"Latent-space world models: Dreamer and RSSM","anchor":"latent-space-world-models-dreamer-and-rssm","text":"Operating models in raw observation space forces prediction of task-irrelevant features (background textures, uninformative pixels) and amplifies prediction error. A more principled approach learns a compact latent state retaining only task-relevant structure."},{"depth":3,"heading":"Recurrent State Space Model (RSSM)","anchor":"recurrent-state-space-model-rssm","text":"The RSSM (Hafner et al., 2019, Dreamer) combines a deterministic recurrent state with a stochastic latent state: The model is trained with a reconstruction loss, a reward prediction loss, and a KL penalty aligning prior and posterior: Planning happens entirely in latent space: RSSM unrolls imagined trajectories using transitions without decoding to observation space — the decoder is only needed for training supervision. Dreamer trains an actor and critic entirely within imagined rollouts using backpropagation through the latent model (analytic policy gradients), achieving state-of-the-art sample efficiency on visual continuous control from pixels (robot manipulation from camera images)."},{"depth":2,"heading":"Monte Carlo Tree Search","anchor":"monte-carlo-tree-search","text":"$2d"},{"depth":2,"heading":"MuZero and planning in learned abstract spaces","anchor":"muzero-and-planning-in-learned-abstract-spaces","text":"$2e"},{"depth":2,"heading":"Synthesis: the model-based RL design space","anchor":"synthesis-the-model-based-rl-design-space","text":"$2f"},{"depth":2,"heading":"GenAI context: planning in language models","anchor":"genai-context-planning-in-language-models","text":"$30"},{"depth":2,"heading":"Browser lab: random-shooting MPC + model error","anchor":"browser-lab-random-shooting-mpc--model-error","text":"1-D point mass: , goal at . MPC re-optimizes each step with a short horizon. A wrong dynamics model ( bias) still works with feedback; open-loop fails. What to try: set modelbias=0.5 or ncand=10 (worse optimization)."},{"depth":2,"heading":"Key takeaways","anchor":"key-takeaways","text":"$31"},{"depth":2,"heading":"Conceptual questions","anchor":"conceptual-questions","text":"$32"},{"depth":2,"heading":"Knowledge Check","anchor":"knowledge-check","text":"Check model-based RL and planning."},{"depth":2,"heading":"Implementation exercises","anchor":"implementation-exercises","text":""},{"depth":3,"heading":"Exercise 1: Dyna on a grid world","anchor":"exercise-1-dyna-on-a-grid-world","text":"Implement a Dyna-Q agent on a deterministic grid world (e.g., FrozenLake-v1 or a custom maze). Compare the number of real environment steps required to converge as a function of planning steps : (standard Q-learning — no imagined updates) (moderate planning) (aggressive planning) Track the Q-value convergence error over real steps. At what value of do diminishing returns set in? What happens when the learned model is initially inaccurate — does the error from imagined updates compound or self-correct? Deliberately introduce model errors by limiting the model table to only store transitions for certain state-action pairs; observe how partial model coverage affects Dyna performance."},{"depth":3,"heading":"Exercise 2: MPC with CEM on a continuous control task","anchor":"exercise-2-mpc-with-cem-on-a-continuous-control-task","text":"Implement receding-horizon MPC using CEM as the optimizer for CartPole-v1 or Pendulum-v1. Your implementation should: Train a deterministic dynamics model from collected transitions. At each step, run CEM for iterations with candidate sequences, horizon . Execute only the first action, then replan. Compare with a model-free baseline (SAC or PPO) on sample efficiency: number of real environment steps to reach a target return. Experiment with horizon lengths . At what does compounding error cause plan quality to degrade? Add a terminal value function and observe whether shorter horizons become competitive — does compensate for model inaccuracy?"},{"depth":3,"heading":"Exercise 3: Latent imagination with a simplified Dreamer-style model","anchor":"exercise-3-latent-imagination-with-a-simplified-dreamer-style-model","text":"Implement a simplified Dreamer-style agent on a visual control task (CarRacing-v3 or a custom pixel environment). The key components: Encoder: CNN encodes observation (deliberately simplified — Dreamer's RSSM keeps a recurrent hidden state; this exercise drops it to isolate the latent-dynamics loop). Dynamics: A learned transition in latent space. Decoder: Reconstructs from for training supervision. Actor/Critic: Trained entirely within imagined latent rollouts of length . Compare with a model-free baseline that operates directly on encoded observations without imagination. Measure the reduction in real environment steps needed to reach a target score. Track the reconstruction quality (MSE between and ) over training — does improved reconstruction correlate with better control performance, or does the latent representation plateau in reconstruction while planning quality continues to improve?"},{"depth":2,"heading":"Extension prompts","anchor":"extension-prompts","text":"$33"},{"depth":2,"heading":"Further reading","anchor":"further-reading","text":"$34"}]}]]}]]}]

With a model, it can ",["$","strong",null,{"style":{"color":"var(--text-strong)","fontWeight":600},"children":"imagine"}]," the consequences of actions\nwithout executing them, generating synthetic experience to supplement or replace real\ninteraction. This is the computational analog of mentally simulating a trajectory before\nmoving — and it matters most for physical systems, where every real interaction carries\na cost or safety risk."]}]

The model can be learned or\ngiven; planning can consume it for one step, a short horizon, or a whole tree of possible\nfutures; and the model can live in observation space or in a learned latent space. We\nwork through that space from simple to complex, anchoring each method to classical\ncontrol theory (MPC, from the robotics course) and to modern systems that plan before\nacting."]}]

This is the open-loop\nprediction problem studied in robust control. rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
goal = 0.0
u_max = 1.0

def true_step(x, u):
    return x + u  # perfect integrator

def model_step(x, u, bias=0.0):
    return x + u + bias  # learned model may be wrong

def cost(traj_x, us):
    return np.sum((traj_x[1:] - goal) ** 2) + 0.05 * np.sum(us**2)

def shoot(x0, H, n_cand, model_bias=0.0):
    best_u, best_c = None, np.inf
    for _ in range(n_cand):
        us = rng.uniform(-u_max, u_max, size=H)
        x = x0
        xs = [x]
        for u in us:
            x = model_step(x, u, bias=model_bias)
            xs.append(x)
        c = cost(np.array(xs), us)
        if c < best_c:
            best_c, best_u = c, us
    return best_u

def run(mpc=True, H=5, model_bias=0.0, T=15, n_cand=80):
    x = 3.0
    xs = [x]
    if not mpc:
        us = shoot(x, T, n_cand, model_bias)
        for u in us:
            x = true_step(x, u)
            xs.append(x)
        return np.array(xs)
    for t in range(T):
        us = shoot(x, H, n_cand, model_bias)
        x = true_step(x, us[0])  # apply first action only
        xs.append(x)
    return np.array(xs)

print(f"{'mode':>14}  {'bias':>6}  {'final |x|':>10}  {'mean |x|':>10}")
for mode, mpc, bias in [
    ("open-loop", False, 0.0),
    ("MPC H=5", True, 0.0),
    ("open + bias", False, 0.25),
    ("MPC + bias", True, 0.25),
]:
    traj = run(mpc=mpc, model_bias=bias)
    print(f"{mode:14s}  {bias:6.2f}  {abs(traj[-1]):10.3f}  {np.mean(np.abs(traj)):10.3f}")

# Horizon effect under perfect model
print("\nMPC horizon vs settling:")
for H in [1, 2, 5, 10]:
    traj = run(mpc=True, H=H, model_bias=0.0, T=12)
    print(f"  H={H:2d}  final|x|={abs(traj[-1]):.3f}")
print("Notice: receding-horizon feedback fixes model bias; pure open-loop compounds planning error.")
